Message from the Governor


                                   The 2025 fall hunting and trapping seasons are just around the corner.
                                   As the long summer days wane and nights become cool, excitement
                                   starts to build for the many New Yorkers who enjoy precious time afeld
                                   hunting or trapping with family and friends or the peace of uncrowded
                                   woods. It’s a time to connect with nature, enjoy the outdoors, and leave
                                   modern distractions behind to ‘Get Ofine and Get Outside.’
                                      New York State ofers an incredible range of opportunity to those
                                   who engage in hunting and trapping. New York's robust environmental
                                   programs, policies, and investments not only help to safeguard and
    preserve our natural resources, but also protects them for generations of New Yorkers to come.
    This ensures that cherished traditions like hunting and trapping remain afordable and accessible
    for all New Yorkers, far into the future.
        This 2025 -26 hunting and trapping syllabus contains all the relevant laws and regulations you
    need to know for a safe, legal, and enjoyable season. It also includes valuable information about
    the work of the New York State Department of Environmental Conservation and our state’s many
    partners working to ofer mentoring, hunter safety, citizen science, and other conservation oppor-
    tunities to help manage and protect our wildlife resources.
        By whatever measure you defne success, enjoy your time afeld this year. Stay safe and enjoy
    the time ofine and out of doors!


    Governor Kathy Hochul

    Message from the Commissioner

                                   Shared Resource,
                                   Shared Responsibility
                                   As DEC’s new Commissioner, I am honored to lead our agency’s
                                   efective stewardship and management of New York’s natural
                                   resources� From Long Island’s coastal pine barrens to unique boreal
                                   forests in the Adirondacks, to the hardwood forests across the
                                   Southern Tier, connecting with these environments is foundational
                                   to who we are� Few activities create and foster those bonds more


                                                                                                            Report Your
                                   than hunting and trapping�
        Spending time in nature with family and friends, or in peaceful solitude, is a value I share with
    the hunters, trappers, and many others who enjoy New York’s lands� The need for protection

                                                                                                            Harvests with the
    and management of our wildlife and our public lands is ever present and a responsibility we all
    share� I am grateful for the cooperation of our sporting community to help address ongoing chal-


                                                                                                            HuntFishNY App
    lenges and provide new opportunities to attract and retain people new to hunting and trapping�
        Over the past year, DEC and our public and private partners helped conserve thousands of
    acres of land, establishing new conservation easements and creating new or expanding existing
    State Forests and Wildlife Management Areas for ecological protection and public recreational
    access� With the expansion of new information-sharing tools like HuntFishNY Events to promote           Use DEC’s HuntFishNY app as
    mentoring opportunities, hunters are helping share their experiences to foster a conservation           an e-license and to report your
    ethic and introduce novice youth and adult hunters to this treasured activity�                          game harvest. HuntFishNY
        DEC looks forward to continuing to build upon our many relationships to protect our shared
    resources and promote the hunting and trapping traditions at the heart of our conservation eforts
                                                                                                            gives you a quick and easy
    in the state� Best of luck to you in the upcoming season, and I hope to see you out and about           way to:
    enjoying the wilds of New York�

8                                                                                                   2025–2026 New York Hunting & Trapping Guide
DEC Wildlife Health Program uses
a “One Health” Approach to Track
and Control Diseases and Toxins
The long-term sustainability of wildlife in New York State is dependent
on the continued health and resilience of wild animal populations� Recent
disease outbreaks have reinforced a critical truth: wildlife health is usually
interconnected with the health of humans, domestic animals, and our
shared environment� A clear example is the ongoing highly pathogenic
avian infuenza (HPAI) or “bird fu” outbreak� This virus is widespread,
afecting many wildlife species including waterfowl, raptors, and some
mammals such as foxes and bobcats� In addition to its impact on wild
species, HPAI has caused signifcant losses in domestic animal indus-
tries, like poultry farms and dairy operations, as well as caused illness
in domestic cats and, in rare cases, humans�
    The DEC Wildlife Health Program (WHP) uses the One Health
approach—a collaborative, multidisciplinary framework that recognizes
                                                                                                                                             John Lehmann
this vitally important intersection of human health, animal health, and the
environment� Understanding wildlife health through a One Health lens
helps guide our response and highlights opportunities for prevention,            cervids, poses a signifcant threat to deer populations and the tradition of
mitigation, and shared stewardship� To this end, the DEC WHP works               hunting� Reporting abnormal behavior is critical for surveillance of CWD
closely with relevant staf at NYS Departments of Agriculture and Markets         and can help maintain resilient deer populations for future generations�
and Health as well as the USDA�                                                      The decisions we make in the feld can have signifcant impacts on
    Hunters, trappers, and other outdoor recreationists play a vital role        both wildlife and human health� For example, using non-lead ammuni-
in maintaining wildlife health in New York� Their time spent in the feld         tion can help prevent lead poisoning in scavenging species such as bald
provides valuable opportunities to observe and report signs of illness,          eagles, as well as reduce the risk of lead exposure in hunter-harvested
contribute to disease surveillance, and support data collection that             game meat� This year’s Hunting and Trapping Regulations Guide features
informs management decisions� For instance, chronic wasting disease              important wildlife health concerns and practical steps you can take to
(CWD), an always-fatal neurological illness afecting deer and other              protect wildlife and your own well-being�

        Reporting Your Game Harvest
            • Deer, Bear, and Turkey harvests must be reported.
            • At press time, DEC proposed regulations to reduce the time period for reporting.
              See DEC's website for updates.
            • You can report your harvest by phone, online, or through our HuntFishNY mobile
              app (see page 8 for details).
            • Reporting online (dec.ny.gov/things-to-do/hunting/report-your-harvest) and
              through the mobile app is simple, fast, and convenient!

Discharge of Firearms,
Crossbows, and Bows
It is illegal to discharge a frearm, crossbow,or bow:
• So that the load or arrow/bolt passes over any
    part of a public highway
• Within 500 feet for a frearm, 250 feet for a
    crossbow, or 150 feet for a bow of any school,
    playground, or an occupied factory or church
• Within 500 feet for a frearm, 250 feet for a
    crossbow, or 150 feet for a bow of a dwelling,
    farm building, or structure in occupation or            Family-owned and operated
    use unless you own it, lease it, are an imme-
    diate member of the family, an employee, or

    have the owner’s consent                                almost 60 years of experience in agricultural implements

                                                                                      Meat processors help ensure that harvested deer and bears
                                                                                      are fully utilized by hunters and their families, or by those in
                                                                                      need through venison donation programs.
                                                                                      Many processors also allow DEC to collect
                                                                                      biological data from deer and bears brought
                                                                                      to their shops, which provides critical infor-
                                                                                      mation to support DEC’s deer and bear man-
        Hunters can help end hunger                                                   agement. To fnd a meat processor near you
                                                                                      please scan this QR code.
             in New York State
          Each year, DEC partners with Feeding New York
          State to provide food for those in need. Through
          a cooperative relationship involving hunters, deer
          processors, the NYS Dept. of Health, and non-proft
          organizations like Feeding NYS, and regional food
          banks, nearly 40 tons of venison are made available
          each year to families across the state.

          There are many ways a hunter can help feed those
          less fortunate. You can donate all or part of your
          deer at a cooperating processor, support your
          regional food bank monetarily, or you can make
          a cash donation to support venison donation
          programs when you purchase your hunting license.


          Learn more about these programs by visiting:
                 dec.ny.gov/things-to-do/hunting/
                   deer-bear/venison-donation

     DEC’s Response to CWD in a
     Captive Deer Herd in 2024
     No CWD Found in Wild Deer
     Chronic Wasting Disease (CWD) is a fatal disease that poses a major           wild white-tailed deer nearby and, if so, to what extent, while AGM was
     threat to New York State's white-tailed deer and moose populations,           responsible for depopulating the infected red deer herd and investigating
     hunting traditions, and sustainable use of venison� It has spread widely      possible sources of origin from other captive deer facilities in the area�
     through deer and elk populations across North America and is caused by        AGM found CWD in 4 of the 200 depopulated red deer but could not
     misfolded proteins, called prions, that afect a deer’s brain and nervous      determine how the red deer herd became infected�
     system� Because there is no vaccine or treatment for CWD, it is almost              DEC’s initial CWD surveillance eforts involved the sampling of wild
     impossible to eliminate once introduced� Therefore, preventing its intro-     deer from within approximately one-half mile of the captive deer facility�
     duction or fnding it early, if present, are the best management strategies�   With cooperating landowner permission, DEC and United States Depart-
         Since 2002, DEC has collected samples from and tested over 69,000         ment of Agriculture, Animal and Plant Health Inspection Service – Wildlife
     deer as part of its annual CWD surveillance program� In 2005, the New         Services (USDA-WS) staf collected 31 deer for testing in early November�
     York State Department of Agriculture and Markets (AGM) detected CWD                 DEC also established an area of interest around the captive deer
     in a captive deer facility and in two wild deer in Oneida County� Through     facility that encompassed the towns of Columbia, German Flatts, Litch-
     swift action by DEC and AGM, including depopulation of the index herd         feld, Warren, and Winfeld in Herkimer County, and the town of Richfeld
     and removal of nearly 300 deer in the area, New York eradicated the CWD       in Otsego County� DEC partnered with hunters, venison processors,
     outbreak, the frst successful eradication of CWD ever documented� For         taxidermists, and highway departments in these towns to collect and
     the next 19 years, New York continued to be CWD-free�                         test samples from hunter-harvested and road-killed deer� To aid in this
         Unfortunately, in the fall of 2024, AGM again detected CWD in a           process, DEC obtained seven deer head collection bins from the Penn-
     captive red deer herd in southern Herkimer County� Upon confrma-              sylvania Game Commission and placed them at convenient locations
     tion, DEC and AGM activated the Interagency CWD Response Plan,                throughout the six-town area to provide an easy avenue for deer heads
     which guides the actions of both agencies to determine the scope of           to be submitted for CWD testing�
     the outbreak and inform future disease management decisions� DEC’s                  DEC collected samples from a total of 201 wild deer over the course
     primary responsibility was to determine whether CWD was present in            of the fall and into early winter� Of those, 196 were submitted for CWD
                                                                                   testing with all determined to be CWD-free� The remaining fve samples
                                                                                   were not able to be tested for varying reasons� These results are encour-
                                                                                   aging and indicate that CWD has not spread into the wild deer herd or,
                                                                                   if it has, the prevalence rate is very low�
                                                                                         While these results are promising, it is premature to claim that New
                                                                                   York is once again CWD-free� There is more work to be done, and you
                                                                                   can help�

                                                                                   If you live or hunt in the six-town area of interest:
                                                                                   1� Immediately report any observations of sick-acting deer to the near-
                                                                                       est DEC ofce� For a listing of DEC ofces and phone numbers, see
                                                                                       page 6�
                                                                                   2� Submit the head of any deer you harvest to DEC for CWD testing� This
                                                                                       will strengthen confdence that New York State remains CWD-free
                                                                                       and reduce the likelihood that DEC will need to collect additional
                                                                                       deer from the area in the future�

                                                                                   DEC also encourages all deer hunters to help prevent the introduction
                                                                                   of CWD into New York State:
                                                                                   • If you deer hunt outside of New York, debone your venison before
                                                                                     bringing it back into New York State as required by law�
                                                                                   • Avoid using natural deer urine-based scents or lures as they could
                                                                                     contain CWD� Use synthetic products instead�
                                                                                   • Dispose of deer carcass parts where the deer was harvested or in a
                                                                                     landfll�
                                                                                   • Hunt only wild deer and support fair chase hunting principles�
                                                                                   • Don’t feed wild deer which can concentrate animals and facilitate the
                                                                                     spread of CWD�



                                                                                   To learn more about CWD and what you can do to help keep
                                                                                   New York State CWD-Free, visit DEC’s CWD webpage at:
                                                                                   https://dec.ny.gov/nature/animals-fsh-plants/wildlife-health/
     CWD head collection bin.                                                      animal-diseases/chronic-wasting-disease


24                                                                                                   2025–2026 New York Hunting & Trapping Guide
  Report Moose Sightings
  DEC and its research partners are working to understand the
  status of the moose population. You can help us by reporting
  moose sightings at dec.ny.gov/nature/animals-fsh-plants/moose.

                                                                                        its junction with Route 28 in the Village of Trenton,

       HuntFishNY Events is your one-stop resource for events ofered
       by DEC and a network of partners and instructors who conduct
       hands- on events to teach you the basics about hunting, fshing,
       trapping, outdoor safety, wildlife, and other outdoor skills.
       Attend an event to learn new skills and join the conservation
       community!
       Topics covered include target shooting, archery, fshing, feld
       dressing, trapping, mentored hunts, sponsored pheasant hunts,
       seminars, public land cleanups, social gatherings, and more!
       Events are designed for all ability levels.

                                                                            Eurasian Boar
                                                                            Eurasian boars are a destructive invasive species that damage habitat and crops, and
                                                                            threaten native wildlife and domestic livestock. DEC and the U.S. Department of Agri-
                                                                            culture (USDA) have worked hard to eradicate these animals from the state’s landscape.
                                                                            We are now working to prevent their reintroduction into New York State.
                                                                             • It is illegal to possess, sell, distribute, trade, or transport Eurasian boars or their hybrids.
                                                                             • It is illegal to import, breed, or release Eurasian boars or their hybrids.
                                                                             • It is illegal to hunt, trap, or take free-ranging Eurasian boar or their hybrids.
                                                                            Although DEC’s eradication eforts have been very successful to date, we must remain
                                                                            vigilant. Anyone who sees a Eurasian boar should report it to DEC as soon as possible
                                                                            by emailing wildlife@dec.ny.gov or calling 518 - 402- 8883.

     Rebate Program
     Lead bullets used for hunting often fragment, leaving small particles
     behind in a gut pile that can sicken or cause death to eagles and other
     scavengers that feed on them� DEC partnered with the NY Coopera-
     tive Fish and Wildlife Research Unit at Cornell University, United States
     Geological Survey, and Conservation Science Global to implement a

                                                                                                                          Deer Hunting

          Doe or Fawn?
          During the fall, it can often be difcult
          to distinguish adult does and fawn deer,
          particularly if seen alone. In recent years,
          about 20% of the total antlerless deer
          harvest has been fawns (5 –7 months old),
          with nearly even proportions of doe                                     Fawn
          fawns and buck fawns. Though fawns
          provide excellent table fare, some hunt-               • Body about as long as tall (square)
          ers prefer to target adult deer. Taking                • Short neck and compact nose/head
          female deer contributes to management
          objectives, and passing buck fawns may                 • Eyes look large for head
          allow some of them to be available later               • Doe fawns have a more rounded head
          as antlered bucks. By learning the difer-                shape between their ears
          ences between antlerless deer, hunters
          can make more informed harvest choices.                • Buck fawns’ heads appear fattened
                                                                   and may have visible antler nubs
                                                                   or “buttons”
          Hunting Tips
           • Button bucks often travel alone,
             but adult does rarely do. Wait until
             several antlerless deer are present
             before making a harvest decision.
           • It is easier to identify sex and age
             when animals are standing still or
             moving slowly.
           • Harvest antlerless deer early in the                              Adult Doe
             season when diferences between
             fawns and adult does are most                       • Body longer than tall (rectangle)
             noticeable.                                         • Long neck and elongated nose

                                                                                          Know the health and species of the tree you choose for your
                                                                                          stand. The following NYS tree species are currently impacted
                                                                                          by non-native pests and pathogens, and should be avoided for
                                                                                          tree stand use: Ash species (Fraxinus spp.), Butternut (Juglans
                                                                                          cineria), American beech (Fagus grandifolia), Eastern hemlock
                                                                                          (Tsuga canadensis), and American elm (Ulmus americana).
                                                                                          Never use a dead, dying, crooked, or leaning tree.

                                        For complete rules and regulations see Official New
                                        York Hunting and Trapping Regulations Guide




                                                                                                                           2025-2026 Hunting & Trapping Seasons Pull-out Summary
                                                               Crossbows may be used to hunt deer and bear during their
                                                               respective firearms seasons (early bear, regular and
                                                               muzzleloading deer & bear seasons). Crossbows may not
                                                               be used to hunt deer during the youth firearms season, or
                                                               anytime in WMUs 1C, 3S, 4J or 8C.

                                                        Long Island & NYC: Nov. 1 – Feb. 25

                                                        Statewide except Long Island & NYC: Oct. 1 – Mar. 29

HPAI and Hunting:
What Waterfowl Hunters
Need to Know
Avian infuenza, or bird fu, is caused by an infuenza type A virus, which are often
carried by wild birds, such as ducks, geese, gulls, and shorebirds� Generally, infuenza
viruses can infect some wild species without causing signs of disease, but new
strains can emerge that cause illness with high mortality in both wild and domestic
birds� These strains are designated as highly pathogenic avian infuenza (HPAI)�
In early 2022, HPAI was detected in wild birds across several eastern           are more likely to come in direct contact with wild, potentially infected
states� In February 2022, the frst case of HPAI in New York was found           birds, as compared to the general population� There are some simple

circulate in bird populations� Some mammals, especially carnivores and          • Harvest only game that appears to be healthy�
scavengers that may eat infected birds, have become ill and died from           • Do not handle sick game—report to your state wildlife agency�
HPAI� Dairy cattle in some states have also been infected with HPAI � The       • Use personal protective equipment like masks, gloves, and eye protec-

        Spruce Grouse vs. Rufed Grouse
        In New York State, the spruce grouse is endangered and is not               Spruce grouse are similar in size and color to rufed grouse, mak-
        legal to hunt. Spruce grouse occur in evergreen forests in the              ing distinguishing between them difcult. One notable diference
        Adirondacks in WMUs 5C, 5F, 6F, and 6J. During the fall, spruce             is that spruce grouse have an orangish-brown band at the tip of
        grouse frequently make their way to roads to eat gravel and often           their tails, which contrasts with rufed grouses’ black tail band.
        travel into upland hardwood forests where rufed grouse occur.               By noting the diferences in the images here, small game hunters
        Small game hunters in the Adirondack region must be able to                 can avoid accidentally shooting a spruce grouse. The loss of a
        distinguish between these species so that spruce grouse are not             single female spruce grouse could be a signifcant setback for a
        shot by mistake.                                                            small local population.

                                                              Protect Rabbits and Hares from RHDV2
dec.ny.gov.                                                   Rabbit hemorrhagic disease virus 2 (RHDV2) is highly lethal to rabbits and hares. It is
                                                              easily transmitted through direct contact between rabbits or contact with contaminated
                                                              objects. RHDV2 is extremely hardy, remaining contagious on surfaces for 3 months. If it
Falconry Seasons                                              enters NYS’s wild rabbit and hare populations, it will be impossible to control and could
                                                              result in signifcant population declines. To protect these species:
To hunt small game species with trained rap-
tors, you must possess a Falconry License and                  • Avoid contact with domestic rabbits.

  Where does DEC stock pheasants?                                                                                                                                                                         Grouse Hunters Needed!
  DEC introduced an interactive mapping tool to display the locations
  where pheasants are released throughout the state. Approximately
  50,000 pheasants are released annually, thanks to the cooperation
  and interest of private and public landowners. Whether on public
  or private property, it's essential to adhere to any special rules and
  respect the land. Scan the QR code to fnd locations using the inter-
  active mapping tool:

       Why Do We Have a May 1 Opener?                                                TICK REMOVAL
                                                                                     • Using tweezers, grab the tick as close to
       Research has found that the optimal start date for the spring                   your skin as possible.
       season is the median date of incubation (when about half of hens
       are on nests). In New York State and much of the Northeast this               • Pull upward with steady, even pressure.
       is around May 1. A May 1 opener provides the best opportunity to              • Clean the bite area with soap and water,
       pursue gobblers when they are vocal and responsive to calling                   rubbing alcohol, or hydrogen peroxide.
       while minimizing disruption to reproduction. An earlier season
       start can disrupt breeding behavior and nesting, negatively                   Check yourself, your children, and pets for ticks daily.
       afecting long-term turkey abundance and hunt quality.

      MIGRATORY BIRD HUNTERS:                                                                                                  Hunting Hours
                                                                                                                               Woodcock may be taken from sunrise to sunset�
                                                                                                                               All other migratory game birds may be taken from

        PROTECT THE GAME, DON’T SKIP HIP                                                                                       one-half hour before sunrise until sunset in all
                                                                                                                               hunting zones� Canada geese may be taken until
                                                                                                                               one-half hour after sunset during the Sept� sea-
                         Te Harvest Information Program (HIP) is a legally                                                     sons, when all other waterfowl hunting seasons
                      required registration for migratory bird hunters of all ages                                             are closed� Otherwise, shooting hours end at sun-
                            that helps biologists manage migratory birds.                                                      set� Snow geese may be taken until one-half hour
                                                                                                                               after sunset during Jan� 15–Apr� 15 whenever
                                                                                                                               all other waterfowl hunting seasons are closed�

                                                                                                                               Non-toxic Shot Requirement
                                                                                                                               Non-toxic shot is required for hunting any migra-
                                                                                                                               tory game bird, except woodcock, everywhere
                                                                                                                               in New York State� Possession or use of shells
                                                                                                                               loaded with shot other than steel, bismuth-tin,
                                                                                                                               iron-tungsten, iron-tungsten-nickel, tungsten-
                                                                                                                               bronze, tungsten-iron-copper-nickel, tungsten-
                                                                                                                               matrix, tungsten-polymer, tungsten-tin-iron,
                                                                                                                               tungsten-tin-bismuth, tungsten-tin-iron-nickel,
                                                                                                                               or other shot approved by the USFWS is strictly
                                                                                                                               prohibited when hunting waterfowl, snipe, rails,
                                                                                                                               or gallinules�

                                                                                                                               Duck Hunting Blinds
                                                                                                                               NYS Navigation Law Section 33-b requires that
                                                                                                                               duck blinds placed in the waters of lakes within
                                                                                                                               New York State be prominently marked with the
                                                                                                                               owner’s full name and address� All duck blinds
                                                                                                                               placed in lakes must be removed no later than
                                                                                                                               Mar� 15 annually�




                        Migratory game birds include ducks, geese, brant, rails,
                               coots, gallinules, woodcock, and snipe.

Route 81 and then south along Interstate Route
81 to the New York-Pennsylvania boundary�

to Route 28, east along Route 28 to Route 29,

Trigger Regulations for Beaver Trappers
This regulation applies if you:
• Trap in a WMU in the Southern Zone during a closed otter season; and                                                  6½" or less
• Use a body-gripping trap larger than 8½ inches (these are “330” size traps)�

2025–2026 New York Hunting & Trapping Guide                                                                                                                 63
     Furbearer Trapping
     Regulations for Body-Gripping Traps Set on Land
     Body-gripping traps set on land shall not be within 100 feet of a public trail except on Wildlife Management Areas�


                                                      A body-gripping trap measuring less than 5½ inches may be set in any manner with or without
                                                      the use of bait� Body-gripping traps measuring 5½ inches to 6 inches, set without the use of bait,
                                                      must be set so that no part of the trap is 8 inches or more above the ground� Body-gripping traps
                                                      measuring more than 6 inches to 7 1/2 inches may never be set in this manner�
                                                          NOTE: During a closed season for fsher and/or marten in the Northern Zone, body-gripping
                                                      traps set on land may not be set with bait or lure�




                                                      Body-gripping traps 5½ inches to 7½ inches set with the
                                                      use of bait, lure, or other attractants may only be used
                                                      as follows:

                                                      ✓Four or more feet of the ground
                                                                       OR



                                                      ✓In a container of the following design:
                                                        • Trap recessed minimum of 18 inches
                                                        • Opening height and width 10 inches or less



                                                                       OR



                                                      ✓In a container of the following design:
                                                        • Only one entrance, facing the ground
                                                        • Container set so entrance is no more than
                                                          6 inches from ground
                                                        • Trap recessed minimum of 4 inches



                                                                       OR



                                                      ✓In a container of the following design:
                                                        • Opening height 6 inches or less
                                                        • Eight-inch minimum spring notches
                                                        • Trap recessed minimum of 4 inches

                                                        You may also build an enclosure of natural
                                                        materials (e�g�, logs or rocks)
                                                        • Opening height 6 inches or less
                                                        • Trap recessed minimum of 8 inches

2025–2026 New York Hunting & Trapping Guide                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      65
     Don’t Eat the Mice!
     Rodenticides in Furbearers
     DEC Seeking Harvested Fisher and Gray Fox Carcasses
     Have you ever used rodenticides to get rid of pesky mice on your prop-
     erty? If so, you’re not alone� The use of rodenticides to control rodents
     has become increasingly common, with anticoagulant rodenticides (ARs)
     being the most frequently used� These compounds inhibit the blood clot-
     ting abilities of rodents, leading to internal bleeding and death� But other
     animals aren’t immune to the impacts� Anything that ingests ARs is at risk�
     Exposure can be through directly ingesting the poison or feeding upon
     another animal that was exposed� Since rodents that consume ARs do not
     die right away and often exhibit slow, abnormal behavior prior to death,
     they make easy prey for hungry wildlife� Large doses of ARs can kill wildlife
     directly by causing them to bleed excessively from relatively minor trauma�
     Smaller doses may not kill them directly, but likely has sublethal impacts like
     making animals more susceptible to diseases or impacting reproduction�
         How common is AR exposure in wildlife? As it turns out – very com-
     mon� Anticoagulant rodenticides have been documented in a wide variety
     of birds and mammals across the U�S� In New York State, researchers at
     SUNY-ESF and Cornell University tested over 100 trapper-harvested fshers
     for the presence of ARs� Over 80% of the fshers were exposed to at least
     one AR compound, with over 50% being exposed to multiple compounds�
     Foxes also seem to be particularly vulnerable, with 100% of red and gray
     fox samples from New Hampshire testing positive for AR presence�

     How can you help?                                                                            Photo courtesy Jack N.           Photo courtesy Eric P.

     DEC has partnered with neighboring state agencies and researchers to
     better understand the impacts of anticoagulant rodenticides and other
     toxins on our wildlife� We will be collecting fresh carcasses of fshers and       Interested in other furbearer-related
     gray foxes to conduct comprehensive health screenings on this fall� If you        work DEC is doing? Check out our
     have a fresh carcass that you are willing to contribute to this study, please
                                                                                       annual Furbearer Newsletter by
     reach out to your Regional Wildlife Ofce or e-mail wildlife@dec�ny�gov
     for more information� Anyone who contributes to the study is eligible to

     receive a Furbearer Management Cooperator patch!

  Report All Poachers
  and Polluters
  1-844 -DEC-ECOS (844 -332-3267)
  Approximately 250 feld ECOs patrol the entire state. Their
  job includes enforcement of fsh and wildlife laws and state
  environmental quality laws. ECOs rely on the eyes and ears of
  our dedicated members of the sporting community to report
  suspected violations. Poachers and other violators are stealing
  from you, your children, and our environment.
  As soon as possible, call the hotline to report suspected viola-
  tions. Dispatchers will assist you in fling a complaint. You may
  ask to have your name kept confdential or fle anonymously.

     Sunrise & Sunset

      Sunrise-Sunset
      Albany, New York


                                                                                                     When Hunting for
                                                                                                     Wildlife, All Shooting
                                                                                                     Hours are Sunrise
                                                                                                     to Sunset, Except:
                                                                                                      • Unprotected wildlife — any hour.
                                                                                                      • Furbearers — after sunrise on the
                                                                                                        opening day of each respective sea-
                                                                                                        son, bobcat, raccoon, skunk, red fox,
                                                                                                        gray fox, opossum, and coyote may
                                                                                                        be taken at any time of day or night.
                                                                                                      • For most migratory game bird
                                                                                                        species, including ducks, geese,
                                                                                                        snipe, rails — ½ hour before sunrise
                                                                                                        to sunset.
                                                                                                      • Spring turkey — ½ hour before sun-
                                                                                                        rise to noon.
                                                                                                      • Big game — ½ hour before sunrise
                                                                                                        to ½ hour after sunset.
